## 7.1.2 Maximum and minimum short-circuit currents

When calculating maximum short-circuit currents, the following conditions apply.

a) Voltage factor $c_{\text{max}}$ according to Table 1 shall be applied for the calculation of maximum short-circuit currents in the absence of a national standard.

b) Choose the system configuration and the maximum contribution from power station units and network feeders which lead to the maximum value of short-circuit current at the short-

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

circuit location, or for accepted sectioning of the network to control the short-circuit current.

c) Impedance correction factors shall be introduced in the positive-, the negative- and the zero-sequence system with exception of the impedances between neutral point and earth.

d) When equivalent impedances $Z_{\mathrm{Q}}$ are used to represent external networks, the minimum equivalent short-circuit impedance shall be used which corresponds to the maximum short-circuit current contribution from the network feeders.

e) Motors shall be included if appropriate in accordance with 6.10.

f) Resistance $R_{\mathrm{L}}$ of lines (overhead lines and cables) shall be introduced at a temperature of $20^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$.

When calculating minimum short-circuit currents, the following conditions apply.

1) Voltage factor $c_{\mathrm{min}}$ for the calculation of minimum short-circuit currents shall be applied according to Table 1.

2) Choose the system configuration and the minimum contribution from power station units and network feeders which lead to a minimum value of short-circuit current at the short-circuit location.

3) The impedance correction factors are equal to 1.

4) Contributions of wind power stations units shall be neglected.

5) Contributions of photovoltaic power station units shall be neglected.

6) Contributions of motors shall be neglected.

7) Resistances $R_{\mathrm{L}}$ of lines (overhead lines and cables, line conductors, and neutral conductors) shall be introduced at a higher temperature:

$$ R_{\mathrm{L}} = \left[1 + \alpha \cdot \left(\beta_{\mathrm{e}} - 20^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\right)\right] \cdot R_{\mathrm{L20}} \tag{32} $$

where

$R_{\mathrm{L20}}$ is the resistance at a temperature of $20^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$;

$\beta_{\mathrm{e}}$ is the conductor temperature in degrees Celsius at the end of the short-circuit duration;

$\alpha$ is a factor equal to 0,004/K, valid with sufficient accuracy for most practical purposes for copper, aluminium and aluminium alloy.

NOTE For $\beta_{\mathrm{e}}$, see for instance IEC 60865-1, IEC 60949 and IEC 60986.

## 7.1.3 Contribution of asynchronous motors to the short-circuit current

Asynchronous motors have to be considered in the calculation of maximum short-circuit current. Low-voltage motors are to be taken into account in auxiliaries of power station units and in industrial and similar installations, for example in networks of chemical and steel industries and pump-stations.

Those high-voltage and low-voltage motors may be neglected, provided that they are not switched in at the same time according to the circuit diagram (interlocking) or to the process (reversible drives).

Low-voltage motors are usually connected to the busbar by cables with different lengths and cross-sections. For simplification of the calculation, groups of motors including their connection cables may be combined to a single equivalent motor.

For these equivalent asynchronous motors

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

$I_{\text{rM}}$ is the sum of the rated currents of all motors in a group of motors (equivalent motor);

$$ I_{\text{LR}} / I_{\text{rM}} = 5; $$

$R_{\text{M}} / X_{\text{M}} = 0.42$ leading to $\kappa_{\text{M}} = 1.3$

$P_{\text{rM}} / p = 0.05 \text{ MW}$ if nothing definite is known, where $p$ is the number of pairs of poles.

In the case of a short circuit on the high-voltage side, the rated current of the transformer may be used for the calculation of $Z_{\text{M}}$ according to Formula (30) instead of the sum of the rated currents $I_{\text{rM}}$ of all motors.

## 7.2 Three-phase initial short-circuit current

### 7.2.1 General

In general, the initial symmetrical short-circuit current $I_{\text{k}}''$ shall be calculated using Formula (33) with the equivalent voltage source $c U_{\text{n}} / \sqrt{3}$ at the short-circuit location and the short-circuit impedance $Z_{\text{k}} = |R_{\text{k}} + \text{j}X_{\text{k}}|$.

$$ I_{\text{k}}'' = \frac{c \cdot U_{\text{n}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot Z_{\text{k}}} = \frac{c \cdot U_{\text{n}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot \sqrt{R_{\text{k}}^{2} + X_{\text{k}}^{2}}} \tag{33} $$

The equivalent voltage source $c U_{\text{n}} / \sqrt{3}$ shall be introduced at the short-circuit location (see Figure 4) with the factor $c$ according to Table 1.

The short-circuit impedance can be found by network reduction or from the diagonal element of the nodal impedance matrix of the positive-sequence system for the node $i$, where the short circuit occurs ($Z_{\text{k}} = Z_{ii}$). The nodal impedance matrix is the inverse of the nodal admittance matrix, see Annex B.

If power station units with full size converter are to be considered, then the maximum initial short-circuit current shall be calculated as follows:

$$ I_{\text{kmax}}'' = \frac{1}{Z_{\text{k}}} \frac{c_{\text{max}} \cdot U_{\text{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} + \frac{1}{Z_{\text{k}}} \sum_{j = 1}^{n} Z_{ij} \cdot I_{\text{skPF}j} = I_{\text{kmaxPFO}}'' + I_{\text{kPF}}'' \tag{34} $$

where

$I_{\text{skPF}j}$ is the rms value of the maximum source current (positive-sequence system) in case of three-phase short-circuit at the high-voltage side of the unit transformer, given by the manufacturer;

$Z_{ii}, Z_{ij}$ are the absolute values of the elements of the nodal impedance matrix of the positive-sequence system, where $i$ is the short-circuit node and $j$ are the nodes where power station units with full size converters are connected (see Annex B);

$I_{\text{kmaxPFO}}''$ is the maximum initial symmetrical short-circuit current without the influence of power station units with full size converter calculated by Formula (33) using $c_{\text{max}}$;

$I_{\text{kPF}}''$ is the sum of the contributions of power station units with full size converter to the initial short-circuit current.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

## 7.2.2 Short-circuit currents inside a power station unit with on-load tap-changer

For calculating the partial short-circuit currents $I_{\mathrm{kG}}^{\prime\prime}$ and $I_{\mathrm{kT}}^{\prime\prime}$ with a short circuit at F1 in Figure 11, in the case of a power station unit with on-load tap-changer, the partial initial symmetrical short-circuit currents are given by:

$$ I_{\mathrm{kG}}^{\prime\prime} = \frac{c_{\max} \cdot U_{\mathrm{rG}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot K_{\mathrm{G},\mathrm{S}} \cdot Z_{\mathrm{G}}} \tag{35} $$

with

$$ K_{\mathrm{G},\mathrm{S}} = \frac{c_{\max}}{1 + x_{\mathrm{d}}^{\prime\prime} \cdot \sqrt{1 - \cos^{2} \varphi_{\mathrm{rG}}}} \tag{36} $$

$$ I_{\mathrm{kT}}^{\prime\prime} = \frac{c_{\max} \cdot U_{\mathrm{rG}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot \left| Z_{\mathrm{TLV}} + \frac{Z_{\mathrm{Qmin}}}{t_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}} \right|} \tag{37} $$

where

$Z_{\mathrm{G}}$ is the subtransient impedance of the generator, see 6.6.1;

$x_{\mathrm{d}}^{\prime\prime}$ is the saturated subtransient reactance referred to the rated impedance: $x_{\mathrm{d}}^{\prime\prime} = X_{\mathrm{d}}^{\prime\prime} / Z_{\mathrm{rG}}$ with $Z_{\mathrm{rG}} = U_{\mathrm{rG}}^{2} / S_{\mathrm{rG}}$;

$Z_{\mathrm{TLV}}$ is the transformer short-circuit impedance referred to the low-voltage side according to 6.3.1, Formulas (7) to (9);

$t_{\mathrm{r}}$ is the rated transformation ratio;

$Z_{\mathrm{Qmin}}$ is the minimum value of the impedance of the network feeder, corresponding to $I_{\mathrm{kQmax}}^{\prime\prime}$.

For $I_{\mathrm{kQmax}}^{\prime\prime}$ the maximum possible value during the lifetime of the power station unit shall be introduced.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

> **[Figura]**: Schematic diagram showing short-circuit currents and partial short-circuit currents for three-phase short circuits in a power station unit. A generator G (3~) connects at busbar F1, from which short-circuit current $\underline{I}''_{kG}$ flows left and $\underline{I}''_{kT}$ flows right through unit transformer T (ratio 1:t) to network Q at voltage $U_{nQ}$, with network impedance $Z_{Qmin}$ and maximum short-circuit current $\underline{I}''_{kQmax}$. At F1, the partial current $\underline{I}''_{kATHV}$ flows downward. Fault location F2 is below F1, from which partial current $\underline{I}''_{kGT}$ feeds into F2 (k3 fault), and $\underline{I}''_{kATHV}$ again flows down through auxiliary transformer AT (ratio $t_{rAT}/1$) to auxiliary busbar A at fault location F3, where short-circuit current $\underline{I}''_{kAT}$ flows. Three-phase fault symbols (k3) are marked at F1, F2, and F3.

**Figure 11 - Short-circuit currents and partial short-circuit currents for three-phase short circuits between generator and unit transformer with or without on-load tap-changer, or at the connection to the auxiliary transformer of a power station unit and at the auxiliary busbar A**

For the calculation of the partial short-circuit current $\underline{I}''_{\mathrm{kGT}}$ feeding into the short-circuit location F2, for example at the connection to the high-voltage side of the auxiliary transformer AT in Figure 11, it is sufficient to take:

$$\underline{I}''_{\mathrm{kGT}} = \frac{c_{\max} \cdot U_{\mathrm{rG}}}{\sqrt{3}} \cdot \left[ \frac{1}{K_{\mathrm{G,S}} \cdot Z_{\mathrm{G}}} + \frac{1}{K_{\mathrm{T,S}} \cdot Z_{\mathrm{TLV}} + \dfrac{Z_{\mathrm{Qmin}}}{t_r^2}} \right] = \frac{c_{\max} \cdot U_{\mathrm{rG}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot Z_{\mathrm{S}}} \tag{38}$$

with

$$K_{\mathrm{T,S}} = \frac{c_{\max}}{1 - x_{\mathrm{T}} \cdot \sqrt{1 - \cos^2 \varphi_{\mathrm{rG}}}} \tag{39}$$

and $K_{\mathrm{G,S}}$ according to Formula (36).

If the unit transformer has an on-load tap-changer on the high-voltage side, it is assumed that the operating voltage at the terminals of the generator is equal to $U_{\mathrm{rG}}$. If, even in this case, the voltage region of the generator $U_{\mathrm{G}} = U_{\mathrm{rG}} (1 \pm p_{\mathrm{G}})$ is used permanently, take Formulas (40) to (41) instead of (35) to (36).

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

The total short-circuit current in F1 or F2 (Figure 11) is found by adding the partial short-circuit current $I_{\mathrm{kATHV}}''$, caused by the medium- and low-voltage auxiliary motors of the power station unit.

## 7.2.3 Short-circuit currents inside a power station unit without on-load tap-changer

For a power station unit without on-load tap-changer of the unit transformer, the partial initial symmetrical short-circuit currents in Figure 11 are given by:

$$ I_{\mathrm{kG}}'' = \frac{c_{\max} \cdot U_{\mathrm{rG}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot K_{\mathrm{G,SO}} \cdot Z_{\mathrm{G}}} \tag{40} $$

with

$$ K_{\mathrm{G,SO}} = \frac{1}{1 + p_{\mathrm{G}}} \cdot \frac{c_{\max}}{1 + x_{\mathrm{d}}'' \cdot \sqrt{1 - \cos^{2} \varphi_{\mathrm{rG}}}} \tag{41} $$

$$ I_{\mathrm{kT}}'' = \frac{c_{\max} \cdot U_{\mathrm{rG}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot \left| Z_{\mathrm{TLV}} + \frac{Z_{\mathrm{Qmin}}}{t_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}} \right|} \tag{42} $$

The partial short-circuit current $I_{\mathrm{kGT}}''$ in Figure 11 can be calculated by:

$$ I_{\mathrm{kGT}}'' = \frac{c_{\max} \cdot U_{\mathrm{rG}}}{\sqrt{3}} \cdot \left[ \frac{1}{K_{\mathrm{G,SO}} \cdot Z_{\mathrm{G}}} + \frac{1}{K_{\mathrm{T,SO}} \cdot Z_{\mathrm{TLV}} + \frac{Z_{\mathrm{Qmin}}}{t_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}}} \right] = \frac{c_{\max} \cdot U_{\mathrm{rG}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot Z_{\mathrm{SO}}} \tag{43} $$

with

$$ K_{\mathrm{T,SO}} = \frac{1}{1 + p_{\mathrm{G}}} \cdot \frac{c_{\max}}{1 - x_{\mathrm{T}} \cdot \sqrt{1 - \cos^{2} \varphi_{\mathrm{rG}}}} \tag{44} $$

and $K_{\mathrm{G,SO}}$ according to Formula (41).

For $Z_{\mathrm{G}}$, $x_{\mathrm{d}}''$, $Z_{\mathrm{TLV}}$, $t_{\mathrm{r}}$ and $Z_{\mathrm{Qmin}}$, see 7.2.2.

The Formula (38) or (43) and the impedance $Z_{\mathrm{S}}$ resp. $Z_{\mathrm{SO}}$ are used to determine the partial short-circuit current $I_{\mathrm{kAT}}''$ in Figure 11 for the short-circuit in F3. The impedance of the auxiliary transformer AT in Figure 11 is to be corrected with $K_{\mathrm{T}}$ from 6.3.3.

The total short-circuit current in F1 or F2 (Figure 11) is found by adding the partial short-circuit current $I_{\mathrm{kATHV}}''$, caused by the high- and low-voltage auxiliary motors of the power station unit.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

## 7.3 Line-to-line short circuit

In the case of a line-to-line short circuit, according to Figure 3b, the initial short-circuit current shall be calculated by:

$$I''_{\mathrm{k2}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{\left| \underline{Z}_{(1)} + \underline{Z}_{(2)} \right|} \cdot \frac{c \cdot U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} \tag{45}$$

If $\underline{Z}_{(2)} = \underline{Z}_{(1)}$, then Formula (45) becomes:

$$I''_{\mathrm{k2}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\, I''_{\mathrm{k}} \tag{46}$$

If power station units with full size converter are to be considered, the maximum initial short-circuit current shall be calculated as follows:

$$I''_{\mathrm{k2max}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{\left| \underline{Z}_{(1)ii} + \underline{Z}_{(2)ii} \right|} \frac{c_{\max} \cdot U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{\left| \underline{Z}_{(1)ii} + \underline{Z}_{(2)ii} \right|} \sum_{j=1}^{n} \underline{Z}_{(1)ij} \cdot I_{(1)\mathrm{sk2PF}j} = I''_{\mathrm{k2maxPFO}} + I''_{\mathrm{k2PF}} \tag{47}$$

where

* $I_{(1)\mathrm{sk2PF}j}$ is the rms value of the maximum source current (positive-sequence system) in case of a line-to-line short circuit at the high-voltage side of the unit transformer, given by the manufacturer; * $\underline{Z}_{(1)ii} = \underline{Z}_{ii}$ is the $i^{\text{th}}$ diagonal element of the nodal impedance matrix of the positive-sequence system, where $i$ is the short-circuit node; * $\underline{Z}_{(2)ii}$ is the $i^{\text{th}}$ diagonal element of the nodal impedance matrix of the negative-sequence system including the impedances of the power station units with full size converter, where $i$ is the short-circuit node; * $\underline{Z}_{(1)ij} = \underline{Z}_{ij}$ are the elements of the nodal impedance matrix of the positive-sequence system, where $i$ is the short-circuit node and $j$ are the nodes where power station units with full size converters are connected; * $I''_{\mathrm{k2maxPFO}}$ is the maximum initial symmetrical short-circuit current calculated by the equivalent voltage source at the short-circuit location without influence of the source currents of the power station units with full size converter; * $I''_{\mathrm{k2PF}}$ is the sum of the contributions of power station units with full size converter to the initial short-circuit current.

## 7.4 Line-to-line short circuit with earth connection

To calculate the initial symmetrical short-circuit currents it is necessary to distinguish between the currents $I''_{\mathrm{k2EL2}}$, $I''_{\mathrm{k2EL3}}$, and $I''_{\mathrm{kE2E}}$ (see Figure 3c).

If $\underline{Z}_{(0)}$ is less than $\underline{Z}_{(2)} = \underline{Z}_{(1)}$, the current $I''_{\mathrm{kE2E}}$ in the line-to-line short circuit with earth connection generally is the largest of all initial symmetrical short-circuit currents $I''_{\mathrm{k}} = I''_{\mathrm{k3}}$, $I''_{\mathrm{k2}}$, $I''_{\mathrm{k2E}}$, and $I''_{\mathrm{k1}}$ (see Figure 7).

The currents $I''_{\mathrm{k2EL2}}$ and $I''_{\mathrm{k2EL3}}$ (see Figure 3c) are calculated according to Formulas (48) and (49):

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

$$ \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{k2EL2}} = \left| \frac{\sqrt{3}\left(\underline{Z}_{(0)} - a\underline{Z}_{(2)}\right)}{\underline{Z}_{(1)}\underline{Z}_{(2)} + \underline{Z}_{(1)}\underline{Z}_{(0)} + \underline{Z}_{(2)}\underline{Z}_{(0)}} \right| \cdot \frac{c U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} \tag{48} $$

$$ \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{k2EL3}} = \left| \frac{\sqrt{3}\left(\underline{Z}_{(0)} - a^{2}\underline{Z}_{(2)}\right)}{\underline{Z}_{(1)}\underline{Z}_{(2)} + \underline{Z}_{(1)}\underline{Z}_{(0)} + \underline{Z}_{(2)}\underline{Z}_{(0)}} \right| \cdot \frac{c U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} \tag{49} $$

The initial short-circuit current $\dot{I}''_{\mathrm{kE2E}}$, flowing to earth and/or grounded wires, according to Figure 3c, is calculated by:

$$ \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{kE2E}} = \left| \frac{3\underline{Z}_{(2)}}{\underline{Z}_{(1)}\underline{Z}_{(2)} + \underline{Z}_{(1)}\underline{Z}_{(0)} + \underline{Z}_{(2)}\underline{Z}_{(0)}} \right| \cdot \frac{c U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} \tag{50} $$

If power station units with full size converter are to be considered, then the maximum initial short-circuit currents shall be calculated as follows.

$$ \begin{aligned} \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{k2EL2max}} &= \left| \frac{\sqrt{3}\left(\underline{Z}_{(0)ii} - a\underline{Z}_{(2)ii}\right)}{\underline{Z}_{(1)ii}\underline{Z}_{(2)ii} + \underline{Z}_{(1)ii}\underline{Z}_{(0)ii} + \underline{Z}_{(2)ii}\underline{Z}_{(0)ii}} \right| \cdot \left(\frac{c_{\max} U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} + \sum_{j=1}^{n} Z_{(1)ij} I_{(1)\mathrm{sk2PF}j}\right) \tag{51} \\ &= \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{k2EL2maxPFO}} + \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{k2EL2PF}} \end{aligned} $$

$$ \begin{aligned} \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{k2EL3max}} &= \left| \frac{\sqrt{3}\left(\underline{Z}_{(0)ii} - a^{2}\underline{Z}_{(2)ii}\right)}{\underline{Z}_{(1)ii}\underline{Z}_{(2)ii} + \underline{Z}_{(1)ii}\underline{Z}_{(0)ii} + \underline{Z}_{(2)ii}\underline{Z}_{(0)ii}} \right| \cdot \left(\frac{c_{\max} U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} + \sum_{j=1}^{n} Z_{(1)ij} I_{(1)\mathrm{sk2PF}j}\right) \tag{52} \\ &= \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{k2EL3maxPFO}} + \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{k2EL3PF}} \end{aligned} $$

$$ \begin{aligned} \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{kE2Emax}} &= \left| \frac{3\underline{Z}_{(2)ii}}{\underline{Z}_{(1)ii}\underline{Z}_{(2)ii} + \underline{Z}_{(1)ii}\underline{Z}_{(0)ii} + \underline{Z}_{(2)ii}\underline{Z}_{(0)ii}} \right| \cdot \left(\frac{c_{\max} U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} + \sum_{j=1}^{n} Z_{(1)ij} I_{(1)\mathrm{sk2PF}j}\right) \tag{53} \\ &= \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{kE2maxPFO}} + \dot{I}''_{\mathrm{kE2EPF}} \end{aligned} $$

where

$I_{(1)\mathrm{sk2PF}j}$ &emsp; is the rms value of the maximum source current (positive-sequence system) in case of a line-to-line short circuit at the high-voltage side of the unit transformer, given by the manufacturer;

$\underline{Z}_{(1)ii} = \underline{Z}_{ii}$ &emsp; is the $i^{\text{th}}$ diagonal element of the nodal impedance matrix of the positive-sequence system, where $i$ is the short-circuit node;

$\underline{Z}_{(2)ii}$ &emsp; is the $i^{\text{th}}$ diagonal element of the nodal impedance matrix of the negative-sequence system including the impedances of the power station units with full size converter, where $i$ is the short-circuit node;

$\underline{Z}_{(0)ii}$ &emsp; is the $i^{\text{th}}$ diagonal element of the nodal impedance matrix of the zero-sequence system, where $i$ is the short-circuit node;

$\underline{Z}_{(1)ij} = \underline{Z}_{ij}$ &emsp; are the elements of the nodal impedance matrix of the positive-sequence system, where $i$ is the short-circuit node and $j$ are the nodes where power station units with full size converters are connected.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

## 7.5 Line-to-earth short circuit

The initial line-to-earth short-circuit current $I_{\mathrm{k1}}''$ in Figure 3d shall be calculated by:

$$I_{\mathrm{k1}}'' = \frac{3}{\left| Z_{(1)} + Z_{(2)} + Z_{(0)} \right|} \cdot \frac{c U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} \tag{54}$$

If $Z_{(0)}$ is less than $Z_{(2)} = Z_{(1)}$, the initial line-to-earth short-circuit current $I_{\mathrm{k1}}''$ is larger than the three-phase short-circuit current $I_{\mathrm{k}}''$, but smaller than $I_{\mathrm{kE2E}}''$ (see Figure 7). However, $I_{\mathrm{k1}}''$ will be the highest current to be interrupted by a circuit breaker if $1{,}0 > Z_{(0)}/Z_{(1)} > 0{,}23$.

If power stations with full size converter are to be considered, then the maximum initial short-circuit current shall be calculated as follows:

$$I_{\mathrm{k1max}}'' = \frac{3}{\left| Z_{(1)ii} + Z_{(2)ii} + Z_{(0)ii} \right|} \cdot \frac{c_{\max} \cdot U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3}} + \frac{3}{\left| Z_{(1)ii} + Z_{(2)ii} + Z_{(0)ii} \right|} \sum_{j=1}^{n} Z_{(1)ij} \cdot I_{(1)\mathrm{sk1PF}_j} = I_{\mathrm{k1maxPFO}}'' + I_{\mathrm{k1PF}}'' \tag{55}$$

where

$I_{(1)\mathrm{sk1PF}_j}$ &nbsp;&nbsp; is the rms value of the maximum source current (positive-sequence system) in case of a line-to-earth short circuit at the high-voltage side of the unit transformer, given by the manufacturer;

$Z_{(1)ii} = Z_{ii}$ &nbsp;&nbsp; is the $i^{\text{th}}$ diagonal element of the nodal impedance matrix of the positive-sequence system, where $i$ is the short-circuit node;

$Z_{(2)ii}$ &nbsp;&nbsp; is the $i^{\text{th}}$ diagonal element of the nodal impedance matrix of the negative-sequence system including the impedances of the power station units with full size converter, where $i$ is the short-circuit node;

$Z_{(0)ii}$ &nbsp;&nbsp; is the $i^{\text{th}}$ diagonal element of the nodal impedance matrix of the zero-sequence system, where $i$ is the short-circuit node;

$Z_{(1)ij} = Z_{ij}$ &nbsp;&nbsp; are the elements of the nodal impedance matrix of the positive-sequence system, where $i$ is the short-circuit node and $j$ are the nodes where power station units with full size converters are connected;

$I_{\mathrm{k1maxPFO}}''$ &nbsp;&nbsp; is the maximum initial symmetrical short-circuit current calculated by the equivalent voltage source at the short-circuit location without influence of the source currents of the power station units with full size converter;

$I_{\mathrm{k1PF}}''$ &nbsp;&nbsp; is the sum of the contributions of wind power station units with full size converter to the initial short-circuit current.

## 8 Calculation of peak short-circuit current

### 8.1 Three-phase short circuit

#### 8.1.1 Single-fed and multiple single-fed short circuits

For single-fed and multiple single-fed three-phase short circuits, the contribution of each source to the peak short-circuit current can be calculated separately and has to be added to the resultant peak short-circuit current. For branches with synchronous generators/motors and asynchronous motors/generators as in Figures 8a to 8d the peak short-circuit current can be calculated by:

$$i_{\mathrm{p}} = \kappa \sqrt{2}\, I_{\mathrm{k}}'' \tag{56}$$

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

In this case, it is assumed that the branches do not include parallel impedances. Networks with branches with parallel impedances are to be treated as multiple-fed short circuits (see 8.1.2).

The factor $\kappa$ for the $R/X$ or $X/R$ ratio shall be obtained from Figure 12 or calculated by the following expression:

$$\kappa = 1{,}02 + 0{,}98\, e^{-3R/X} \tag{57}$$

> **[Figura]**: Figure 12 consists of two side-by-side graphs plotting the factor $\kappa$ (vertical axis, ranging from 1,0 to 2,0) against two different horizontal axes. The left graph shows $\kappa$ as a decreasing curve versus the ratio $R/X$ (ranging from 0 to 1,2): the curve starts near 2,0 at $R/X = 0$ and decays smoothly toward approximately 1,0 as $R/X$ approaches 1,2. The right graph shows $\kappa$ as an increasing S-shaped (sigmoidal) curve versus the ratio $X/R$ (ranging from 0,5 to 200, plotted on a logarithmic scale): the curve starts near 1,0 at low $X/R$ values and rises asymptotically toward 2,0 at high $X/R$ values. Both graphs are overlaid on a uniform grid, and the label "IEC" appears at the bottom right.

**Figure 12 - Factor $\kappa$ for series circuit as a function of ratio $R/X$ or $X/R$**

In case of branches with synchronous generators and power station units, the following values for the fictitious resistances $R_{\mathrm{Gf}}$ are to be used for the calculation of the peak short-circuit current with sufficient accuracy.

$$R_{\mathrm{Gf}} = 0{,}05 \cdot X_{\mathrm{d}}'' \quad \text{for generators with } U_{\mathrm{rG}} > 1\ \text{kV and } S_{\mathrm{rG}} \geq 100\ \text{MVA}$$

$$R_{\mathrm{Gf}} = 0{,}07 \cdot X_{\mathrm{d}}'' \quad \text{for generators with } U_{\mathrm{rG}} > 1\ \text{kV and } S_{\mathrm{rG}} < 100\ \text{MVA}$$

$$R_{\mathrm{Gf}} = 0{,}15 \cdot X_{\mathrm{d}}'' \quad \text{for generators with } U_{\mathrm{rG}} \leq 1000\ \text{V}$$

In addition to the decay of the DC component, the factors $0{,}05$, $0{,}07$, and $0{,}15$ also take into account the decay of the AC component of the short-circuit current during the first half-cycle after the short circuit took place. The influence of various winding-temperatures on $R_{\mathrm{Gf}}$ is not considered.

The values $R_{\mathrm{Gf}}$ should be used for the calculation of the peak short-circuit current. These values cannot be used when calculating the aperiodic component $i_{\mathrm{DC}}$ of the short-circuit current according to Formula (76). The effective resistance of the stator of synchronous machines lies generally much below the given values for $R_{\mathrm{Gf}}$. In this case the manufacturer's values for $R_{\mathrm{G}}$ should be used.

For one power station unit with full size converter, according to Figures 8f and 8g, the peak short-circuit current is determined by:

$$i_{\mathrm{p}} = \sqrt{2}\, I_{\mathrm{kPF}}'' = \sqrt{2}\, I_{\mathrm{skPF}}'' \tag{58}$$

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

The peak short-circuit current $i_{\mathrm{p}}$ at a short-circuit location F is the sum of the partial short-circuit currents:

$$ i_{\mathrm{p}} = \sum_{i} i_{\mathrm{p}i} \tag{59} $$

Figure 9 example:

$$ i_{\mathrm{p}} = i_{\mathrm{pS}} + i_{\mathrm{pT}} + i_{\mathrm{pWF}} + i_{\mathrm{pM}} \tag{60} $$

## 8.1.2 Multiple-fed short circuit

For calculating the peak short-circuit current $i_{\mathrm{p}}$ the following formula shall be used:

$$ i_{\mathrm{p}} = \kappa\sqrt{2} \cdot I''_{\mathrm{kmaxPFO}} + \sqrt{2} \cdot I''_{\mathrm{kPF}} \tag{61} $$

where

$I''_{\mathrm{kmaxPFO}}$ &emsp; is the maximum initial three-phase short-circuit current without influence of power station units with full size converter calculated by Formula (33);

$I''_{\mathrm{kPF}}$ &emsp; is the contribution of the power station units with full size converter calculated by Formula (34).

The factor $\kappa$ shall be calculated by Formula (57) with one of the following methods a), b), or c) for determination of the ratio $R/X$. Method c) is recommended (see IEC TR 60909-1).

### a) Uniform ratio $R/X$ or $X/R$

For this method the smallest ratio of $R/X$ or the largest ratio of $X/R$ of all branches of the network is taken.

It is necessary to choose only the branches which carry partial short-circuit currents at the nominal voltage corresponding to the short-circuit location and branches with transformers feeding the voltage level of the short-circuit location. Any branch may be a series combination of several impedances.

### b) Ratio $R/X$ or $X/R$ at the short-circuit location

For this method, the ratio $R_{\mathrm{k}}/X_{\mathrm{k}}$ given by the short-circuit impedance $Z_{\mathrm{k}} = R_{\mathrm{k}} + \mathrm{j}X_{\mathrm{k}}$ (seen from the short-circuit location) of the initial short-circuit calculation is used.

To cover inaccuracies, the factor $\kappa$ determined with the ratio $R_{\mathrm{k}}/X_{\mathrm{k}}$ is multiplied by a factor 1,15.

As long as $R/X$ remains smaller than 0,3 in all branches which carry a short-circuit current, it is not necessary to use the factor 1,15. It is also not necessary for the product $1{,}15 \cdot \kappa$ to exceed 1,8 in low-voltage networks or to exceed 2,0 in high-voltage networks.

### c) Equivalent frequency $f_{\mathrm{c}}$

An equivalent impedance $Z_{\mathrm{c}} = R_{\mathrm{c}} + \mathrm{j}X_{\mathrm{c}}$ of the positive-sequence system as seen from the short-circuit location is calculated assuming a frequency $f_{\mathrm{c}} = 20\text{ Hz}$ (for a nominal frequency of $f = 50\text{ Hz}$) or $f_{\mathrm{c}} = 24\text{ Hz}$ (for a nominal frequency of $f = 60\text{ Hz}$). The $R/X$ or $X/R$ ratio is then determined according to Formula (62).

$$ \frac{R}{X} = \frac{R_{\mathrm{c}}}{X_{\mathrm{c}}} \cdot \frac{f_{\mathrm{c}}}{f} \qquad \frac{X}{R} = \frac{X_{\mathrm{c}}}{R_{\mathrm{c}}} \cdot \frac{f}{f_{\mathrm{c}}} \tag{62} $$

where

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

$\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{c}} = R_{\mathrm{c}} + \mathrm{j}X_{\mathrm{c}}$ is the equivalent impedance of the system as seen from the short-circuit location for the assumed frequency $f_{\mathrm{c}}$;

$R_{\mathrm{c}}$ is the real part of $\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{c}}$ ($R_{\mathrm{c}}$ is generally not equal to the $R$ at nominal frequency);

$X_{\mathrm{c}}$ is the imaginary part of $\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{c}}$ ($X_{\mathrm{c}}$ is generally not equal to the $X$ at nominal frequency).

When using this method with transformers, generators and power station units, the impedance correction factors $K_{\mathrm{T}}$, $K_{\mathrm{G}}$ and $K_{\mathrm{S}}$, respectively $K_{\mathrm{SO}}$, shall be introduced with the same values as for the 50 Hz or 60 Hz calculations.

## 8.2 Line-to-line short circuit

For a line-to-line short circuit in a network with power station units with full size converter, the peak short-circuit current can be expressed by:

$$ i_{\mathrm{p2}} = \kappa \sqrt{2} I''_{\mathrm{k2maxPFO}} + \sqrt{2} I''_{\mathrm{k2PF}} \tag{63} $$

where

$I''_{\mathrm{k2maxPFO}}$ is the maximum initial line-to-line short-circuit current without influence of the source currents of power station units with full size converter calculated according to Formula (47);

$I''_{\mathrm{k2PF}}$ is the contribution of the power station units with full size converter calculated according to Formula (47).

The factor $\kappa$ shall be calculated according to 8.1 depending on the system configuration.

## 8.3 Line-to-line short circuit with earth connection

For a line-to-line short circuit with earth connection, the peak short-circuit current (e.g. of line L2) can be expressed by:

$$ i_{\mathrm{p2EL2}} = \kappa \sqrt{2} I''_{\mathrm{k2EL2maxPFO}} + \sqrt{2} I''_{\mathrm{k2EL2PF}} \tag{64} $$

where

$I''_{\mathrm{k2EL2maxPFO}}$ is the maximum initial line-to-line short-circuit current without influence of the source currents of power station units with full size converter calculated according to Formula (51);

$I''_{\mathrm{k2EL2PF}}$ is the contribution of the power station units with full size converter calculated according to Formula (51).

The factor $\kappa$ shall be calculated according to 8.1 depending on the system configuration.

## 8.4 Line-to-earth short circuit

For a line-to-earth short circuit, the peak short-circuit current can be expressed by:

$$ i_{\mathrm{p1}} = \kappa \sqrt{2} I''_{\mathrm{k1maxPFO}} + \sqrt{2} I''_{\mathrm{k1PF}} \tag{65} $$

where

$I''_{\mathrm{k1maxPFO}}$ is the maximum initial line-to-earth short-circuit current without influence of the source currents of power station units with full size converter calculated according to Formula (55);

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

$I_{\text{k1PF}}^{''}$ is the contribution of the power station units with full size converter calculated according to Formula (55).

The factor $\kappa$ shall be calculated according to 8.1 depending on the system configuration.

# 9 Calculation of symmetrical breaking current

## 9.1 Three-phase short circuit

### 9.1.1 Symmetrical breaking current of synchronous machines

For a near-to-generator short circuit, in the case of a single-fed short circuit as in Figure 8b, 8c and 8d or multiple single-fed short circuit as in Figure 9, the decay to the symmetrical short-circuit breaking current of a synchronous generator is taken into account by the factor $\mu$ according to Formula (67).

$$I_{\text{b}} = \mu \cdot I_{\text{kmax}}^{''} \tag{66}$$

The factor $\mu$ depends on the minimum time delay $t_{\min}$ and the ratio $I_{\text{kG}}^{''} / I_{\text{rG}}$, where $I_{\text{rG}}$ is the rated generator current. The values of $\mu$ in Formula (67) apply if synchronous machines are excited by rotating exciters or by static converter exciters (provided, for static exciters, the minimum time delay $t_{\min}$ is less than 0,25 s and the maximum excitation voltage is less than 1,6 times the rated load excitation-voltage). For all other cases take $\mu = 1$, if the exact value is unknown.

> **NOTE** The AC component of the current in case of near-to-generator short circuits may decay faster than the DC component. The magnitude of the AC component of the current depends upon the operating conditions of the generator before short circuit; the decay of the AC component is governed by the subtransient and transient time constants, the decay of the DC component by the armature time constant. As a consequence, the DC component during a certain period of time can be higher than the peak value of the AC component. In such a case the fault current shows delayed current zero. In order to evaluate if power station equipment can handle this current, the AC and DC components of the short-circuit current have to be calculated taking into account the synchronous, transient and subtransient reactances, the transient and subtransient time constants, the circuit-breaker arc resistance as well as the operation condition before short circuit.

When there is a unit transformer between the generator and the short-circuit location, the partial short-circuit current $I_{\text{kS}}^{''}$ at the high-voltage side of the unit transformer (in Figure 8c) shall be transferred by the rated transformation ratio to the terminal of the generator $I_{\text{kG}}^{''} = t_{\text{r}} \cdot I_{\text{kS}}^{''}$ before calculating $\mu$, using the following formulas:

$$\begin{aligned} \mu &= 0{,}84 + 0{,}26\, e^{-0{,}26\, I_{\text{kG}}^{''} / I_{\text{rG}}} \quad \text{for} \quad t_{\min} = 0{,}02 \text{ s} \\ \mu &= 0{,}71 + 0{,}51\, e^{-0{,}30\, I_{\text{kG}}^{''} / I_{\text{rG}}} \quad \text{for} \quad t_{\min} = 0{,}05 \text{ s} \\ \mu &= 0{,}62 + 0{,}72\, e^{-0{,}32\, I_{\text{kG}}^{''} / I_{\text{rG}}} \quad \text{for} \quad t_{\min} = 0{,}10 \text{ s} \\ \mu &= 0{,}56 + 0{,}94\, e^{-0{,}38\, I_{\text{kG}}^{''} / I_{\text{rG}}} \quad \text{for} \quad t_{\min} \geq 0{,}25 \text{ s} \end{aligned} \tag{67}$$

If $I_{\text{kG}}^{''} / I_{\text{rG}}$ is not greater than 2, apply $\mu = 1$ for all values of the minimum time delay $t_{\min}$. The factor $\mu$ may also be obtained from Figure 13. For other values of minimum time delay, linear interpolation between curves is acceptable.

Figure 13 can be used also for compound excited low-voltage generators with a minimum time delay $t_{\min}$ not greater than 0,1 s. The calculation of low-voltage breaking currents after a time delay $t_{\min}$ greater than 0,1 s is not included in this standard; generator manufacturers may be able to provide information.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

> **[Figura 13]**: Graph showing factor $\mu$ (y-axis, ranging from 0,5 to 1,0) as a function of the ratio of symmetrical short-circuit current to rated current for three-phase short circuit ($\dot{I}_{\mathrm{kG}}/I_{\mathrm{rG}}$ or $\dot{I}_{\mathrm{kM}}/I_{\mathrm{rM}}$, x-axis ranging from 0 to 9). Four descending curves are shown, each corresponding to a minimum time delay $t_{\mathrm{min}}$: 0,02 s (highest curve), 0,05 s, 0,10 s, and ≥ 0,25 s (lowest curve). All curves start at $\mu = 1{,}0$ near ratio = 2 and decrease monotonically, converging toward values between 0,6 and 0,85 at ratio = 9.

**Figure 13 - Factor $\mu$ for calculation of short-circuit breaking current $I_{\mathrm{b}}$**

## 9.1.2 Symmetrical breaking current of asynchronous machines

The symmetrical short-circuit breaking current of an asynchronous machine shall be calculated by Formula (68) with factor $\mu$ according to Formula (67) or Figure 13 using $\dot{I}_{\mathrm{kM}} / I_{\mathrm{rM}}$ (see Table 4) and the factor $q$.

$$I_{\mathrm{b}} = \mu \cdot q \cdot \dot{I}_{\mathrm{kmax}} \tag{68}$$

Formula (67) shall also be used for the calculation of the symmetrical short-circuit breaking current of wind power stations with asynchronous generator with $\dot{I}_{\mathrm{kWA}}$.

The factor $q$ may be determined as a function of the minimum time delay $t_{\mathrm{min}}$ or may be taken from Figure 14.

$$\begin{aligned} q &= 1{,}03 + 0{,}12 \ln \left(P_{\mathrm{rM}} / p\right) && \text{for } t_{\mathrm{min}} = 0{,}02\text{ s} \\ q &= 0{,}79 + 0{,}12 \ln \left(P_{\mathrm{rM}} / p\right) && \text{for } t_{\mathrm{min}} = 0{,}05\text{ s} \\ q &= 0{,}57 + 0{,}12 \ln \left(P_{\mathrm{rM}} / p\right) && \text{for } t_{\mathrm{min}} = 0{,}10\text{ s} \\ q &= 0{,}26 + 0{,}10 \ln \left(P_{\mathrm{rM}} / p\right) && \text{for } t_{\mathrm{min}} \geq 0{,}25\text{ s} \end{aligned} \tag{69}$$

where

$P_{\mathrm{rM}}$ is the rated active power in MW and $p$ is the number of pairs of poles of the asynchronous machine.

If the calculation in Formula (69) provides larger values than 1 for $q$, assume that $q = 1$.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

> **[Figura]**: Graph of factor $q$ (vertical axis, ranging from 0 to 1,0) versus active power of the motor per pair of poles $P_\mathrm{M}/p$ (horizontal axis, logarithmic scale from 0,01 MW to 10 MW). Four diagonal lines rise from lower-left to upper-right, each corresponding to a minimum time delay $t_\mathrm{min}$: 0,02 s (uppermost line), 0,05 s, 0,10 s, and ≥ 0,25 s (lowest line). Higher power per pole pair and shorter time delay yield larger values of $q$.

**Figure 14 - Factor $q$ for the calculation of the symmetrical short-circuit breaking current of asynchronous motors**

## 9.1.3 Symmetrical breaking current of power station units with doubly fed asynchronous generator

The symmetrical breaking current of power station units with doubly fed asynchronous generators can be calculated according to Formula (70).

$$I_{\mathrm{b}} = \mu_{\mathrm{WD}} \cdot I''_{\mathrm{kWD}} \tag{70}$$

with

$$\mu_{\mathrm{WD}} = \frac{I_{\mathrm{kWDmax}}}{I''_{\mathrm{kWD}}} \tag{71}$$

$I_{\mathrm{kWDmax}}$ is the maximum steady state short-circuit current, given by the manufacturer (see 11.2.3).

## 9.1.4 Symmetrical breaking current of power station units with full size converter

The symmetrical breaking current of power station units with full size converters can be calculated according to Formula (72).

$$I_{\mathrm{b}} = I_{\mathrm{kPFmax}} \tag{72}$$

where

$I_{\mathrm{kPFmax}}$ is the maximum steady state short-circuit current, see 11.2.4.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

## 9.1.5 Symmetrical breaking current of network feeder

The symmetrical breaking current of network feeders can be calculated according to Formula (73).

$$I_{\mathrm{b}} = I_{\mathrm{kmax}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{73}$$

## 9.1.6 Symmetrical breaking current in case of multiple single-fed short-circuits

For multiple single-fed three-phase short circuits as in Figure 9, the symmetrical breaking current at the short-circuit location can be calculated by the summation of the individual breaking current contributions:

$$I_{\mathrm{b}} = \sum_{i} I_{\mathrm{b}i} \tag{74}$$

Figure 9 example:

$$I_{\mathrm{b}} = I_{\mathrm{bS}} + I_{\mathrm{bT}} + I_{\mathrm{bWF}} + I_{\mathrm{bM}} = \mu \cdot I_{\mathrm{kS}}^{\prime\prime} + I_{\mathrm{kT}}^{\prime\prime} + I_{\mathrm{kWFmax}}^{\prime\prime} + \mu \cdot q \cdot I_{\mathrm{kM}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{75}$$

where

$I_{\mathrm{kS}}^{\prime\prime}$, $I_{\mathrm{kT}}^{\prime\prime}$, $I_{\mathrm{kWF}}^{\prime\prime}$ and $I_{\mathrm{kM}}^{\prime\prime}$ are taken as its contributions to $I_{\mathrm{kmax}}^{\prime\prime}$ at the short-circuit location.

At first the maximum short-circuit current is calculated, and then the partial currents in the branches where the circuit breakers are located.

## 9.1.7 Symmetrical breaking current in case of multiple-fed short circuits

In case of multiple-fed short circuits, current $I_{\mathrm{b}}$ can be calculated by:

$$I_{\mathrm{b}} = I_{\mathrm{kmax}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{76}$$

Currents calculated with Formula (76) are larger than the real symmetrical short-circuit breaking currents. For increased accuracy, Formula (77) can be used.

$$\begin{aligned} \underline{I}_{\mathrm{b}} = {} & \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kmax}}^{\prime\prime} - \sum_{i} \frac{\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{GK}i} \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kG}i}^{\prime\prime}}{c U_{\mathrm{n}} / \sqrt{3}} \cdot (1 - \mu_{i}) \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kG}i}^{\prime\prime} - \sum_{j} \frac{\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{M}j} \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kM}j}^{\prime\prime}}{c U_{\mathrm{n}} / \sqrt{3}} \cdot (1 - \mu_{j} q_{j}) \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kM}j}^{\prime\prime} \\ & - \sum_{k} \frac{\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{SK}k} \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kS}k}^{\prime\prime}}{c U_{\mathrm{n}} / \sqrt{3}} \cdot (1 - \mu_{k}) \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kS}k}^{\prime\prime} - \sum_{l} \frac{\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{SOK}l} \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kSOl}}^{\prime\prime}}{c U_{\mathrm{n}} / \sqrt{3}} \cdot (1 - \mu_{l}) \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kSOl}}^{\prime\prime} \\ & - \sum_{m} \frac{\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{WA}m} \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kWA}m}^{\prime\prime}}{c U_{\mathrm{n}} / \sqrt{3}} \cdot (1 - \mu_{m}) \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kWA}m}^{\prime\prime} - \sum_{n} \frac{\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{WD}n} \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kWD}n}^{\prime\prime}}{c U_{\mathrm{n}} / \sqrt{3}} \cdot (1 - \mu_{n}) \cdot \underline{I}_{\mathrm{kWD}n}^{\prime\prime} \end{aligned} \tag{77}$$

where

$\mu_i$, $\mu_j$, $\mu_k$, $\mu_l$, $\mu_m$ are calculated with Formula (67);

$\mu_n$ is calculated with Formula (71);

$q_j$ is calculated with Formula (69);

$c U_{\mathrm{n}} / \sqrt{3}$ is the equivalent voltage source at the short-circuit location;

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

$\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{GK}i}$ &emsp; is the corrected short-circuit impedance of the $i^{\text{th}}$ synchronous machine;

$\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{M}j}$ &emsp; is the short-circuit impedance of the $j^{\text{th}}$ asynchronous motor;

$\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{SK}k}$ &emsp; is the corrected short-circuit impedance of the $k^{\text{th}}$ power station unit with on-load tap-changer;

$\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{SOK}l}$ &emsp; is the corrected short-circuit impedance of the $l^{\text{th}}$ power station unit without on-load tap-changer;

$\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{WA}m}$ &emsp; is the short-circuit impedance of the $m^{\text{th}}$ wind power station unit with asynchronous generator;

$\underline{Z}_{\mathrm{WD}n}$ &emsp; is the short-circuit impedance of the $n^{\text{th}}$ wind power station unit with doubly-fed asynchronous generator;

$I_{\mathrm{kG}i}^{\prime\prime}$, $I_{\mathrm{kM}j}^{\prime\prime}$ &emsp; are the contributions from the $i^{\text{th}}$ synchronous generator or the $j^{\text{th}}$ asynchronous motor;

$I_{\mathrm{kS}k}^{\prime\prime}$, $I_{\mathrm{kSO}l}^{\prime\prime}$ &emsp; are the contributions from the $k^{\text{th}}$ power station unit with on-load tap-changer or the $l^{\text{th}}$ power station unit without on-load tap-changer;

$I_{\mathrm{kWA}m}^{\prime\prime}$, $I_{\mathrm{kWD}n}^{\prime\prime}$ &emsp; are the contributions from the $m^{\text{th}}$ wind power station unit with asynchronous generator or from the $n^{\text{th}}$ wind power station unit with doubly fed asynchronous generator;

$c$ &emsp; is the voltage factor according to Table 1 ($c_{\max}$).

The partial short-circuit currents and the impedances of Formula (77) shall be related to the voltage level $U_{\mathrm{n}}$, where the short-circuit current is calculated.

If $\mu_j = 1$, then take $1 - \mu_j \cdot q_j = 0$, independent of the value $q_j$.

## 9.2 Unbalanced short-circuits

The short-circuit breaking current is assumed to be equal to the initial short-circuit currents:

$$I_{\mathrm{b2}} = I_{\mathrm{k2max}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{78}$$

$$I_{\mathrm{b2E}} = I_{\mathrm{k2Emax}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{79}$$

$$I_{\mathrm{b1}} = I_{\mathrm{k1max}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{80}$$

NOTE &emsp; For near-to-generator short circuits, the flux decay in the generator is not taken into account.

## 10 DC component of the short-circuit current

The maximum DC component $i_{\mathrm{DC}}$ of the short-circuit current may be calculated with sufficient accuracy by Formula (81).

$$i_{\mathrm{DC}} = \sqrt{2} \cdot I_{\mathrm{k}}^{\prime\prime} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{-2\pi \cdot f \cdot t \cdot R/X} \tag{81}$$

where

$I_{\mathrm{k}}^{\prime\prime}$ &emsp; is the initial symmetrical short-circuit current;

$f$ &emsp; is the nominal frequency;

$t$ &emsp; is the time;

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

$R/X$ is the ratio according to 8.1 or the ratios according to the methods a) and c) in 8.1.

The correct resistance $R_{\mathrm{G}}$ of the generator armature should be used and not $R_{\mathrm{Gf}}$.

Power station units with full converter are neglected when calculating the DC component of the short-circuit current.

For multiple-fed or single-fed with parallel branches, the ratio $R/X$ or $X/R$ is to be determined by the method c) in 8.1. Depending on the product $f \cdot t$, where $f$ is the frequency and $t$ is the time, the equivalent frequency $f_{\mathrm{c}}$ should be used as follows:

| $f \cdot t$ | $< 1$ | $< 2{,}5$ | $< 5$ | $< 12{,}5$ | | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | | $f_{\mathrm{c}}/f$ | 0,27 | 0,15 | 0,092 | 0,055 |

For example, the DC component of the short-circuit current is used to calculate the asymmetrical breaking current $I_{\mathrm{basyn}}$ (rms value) according to Formula (82).

$$ I_{\mathrm{basyn}} = \sqrt{I_{\mathrm{b}}^{2} + i_{\mathrm{DC}}^{2}} = I_{\mathrm{b}} \sqrt{1 + \left(\frac{i_{\mathrm{DC}}}{I_{\mathrm{b}}}\right)^{2}} \tag{82} $$

where

$I_{\mathrm{b}}$ is the symmetrical breaking current;

$i_{\mathrm{DC}}$ is the DC component of a short-circuit current depending on the minimum time delay, see Formula (81).

## 11 Calculation of steady-state short-circuit current

### 11.1 General

The calculation of the steady-state short-circuit current $I_{\mathrm{k}}$ is less accurate than the calculation of the initial short-circuit current $I_{\mathrm{k}}''$.

### 11.2 Three-phase short circuit

#### 11.2.1 Steady-state short-circuit current of one synchronous generator or one power station unit

##### 11.2.1.1 General

For near-to-generator three-phase short circuits fed directly from one synchronous generator or one power station unit only, according to Figure 8b or 8d, the steady-state short-circuit current $I_{\mathrm{k}}$ depends on the excitation system, the voltage regulator action, and saturation influences.

Synchronous machines (generators, motors, or compensators) with terminal-fed static exciters do not contribute to $I_{\mathrm{k}}$ in the case of a short circuit at the terminals of the machine, but they contribute to $I_{\mathrm{k}}$ if there is an impedance between the terminals and the short-circuit location. A contribution is also given if, in case of a power station unit, the short circuit occurs on the high-voltage side of the unit transformer (see Figure 8d).

All other cases have to be treated according to 11.2.7.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

## 11.2.1.2 Maximum steady-state short-circuit current

For the calculation of the maximum steady-state short-circuit current, the synchronous generator may be set at the maximum excitation.

$$ I_{\mathrm{kmax}} = \lambda_{\max} \cdot I_{\mathrm{rG}} \tag{83} $$

For static excitation systems fed from the generator terminals and a short circuit at the terminals, the field voltage collapses as the terminal voltage collapses, therefore take $\lambda_{\max} = \lambda_{\min}$ in this case.

When calculating $I_{\mathrm{kmax}}$ or $I_{\mathrm{kmin}}$, the factor $c_{\max}$ or $c_{\min}$ is taken from Table 1.

$\lambda_{\max}$ may be obtained from Figures 15 or 16 for cylindrical rotor generators or salient-pole generators. The saturated reactance $x_{\mathrm{dsat}}$ is the reciprocal of the saturated no-load short-circuit ratio.

$\lambda_{\max}$-curves of series 1 are based on the highest possible excitation voltage according to either 1,3 times the rated excitation at rated apparent power and power factor for cylindrical rotor generators (Figure 15a) or 1,6 times the rated excitation voltage for salient-pole generators (Figure 16a).

$\lambda_{\max}$-curves of series 2 are based on the highest possible excitation voltage according to either 1,6 times the rated excitation at rated apparent power and power factor for cylindrical rotor generators (Figure 15b), or 2,0 times the rated excitation voltage for salient-pole generators (Figure 16b).

$\lambda_{\max}$-curves of series 1 or 2 may also be applied in the case of terminal-fed static exciters, if the short circuit is at the high-voltage side of the unit transformer of a power station unit or in the system, and if the maximum excitation voltage is chosen with respect to the partial breakdown of the terminal voltage of the generator during the short circuit.

NOTE The calculation of the $\lambda_{\max}$-curves is possible with Formula (87) from IEC TR 60909-1:2002, taking into account that $I^*_{\mathrm{kG}}/I_{\mathrm{rG}} = \lambda_{\max}$ is valid for ratios $I^*_{\mathrm{kG}}/I_{\mathrm{rG}} \le 2$. This occurs in the case of a far-from-generator short circuit.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

![img-32.jpeg](img-32.jpeg)

![img-33.jpeg](img-33.jpeg)

## 11.2.1.3 Minimum steady-state short-circuit current

For the minimum steady-state short-circuit current in the case of a single-fed short circuit from one generator or one power station unit according to Figures 8b and 8c, constant no-load excitation (voltage regulator not being effective) of the synchronous machine is assumed:

$$ I_{\text{kmin}} = \lambda_{\text{min}} \cdot I_{\text{rG}} \tag{84} $$

$\lambda_{\text{min}}$ may be obtained from Figures 15 and 16. In the case of minimum steady-state short circuit introduce $c = c_{\text{min}}$, according to Table 1.

The calculation of the minimum steady-state short-circuit current in the case of a near-to-generator short circuit, fed by one or several similar and parallel working generators with compound excitation, is made as follows:

$$ I_{\text{kmin}} = \frac{c_{\text{min}} \cdot U_{\text{n}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot Z_{\text{k}}} \tag{85} $$

For the effective reactance of generators introduce:

$$ X_{\text{dP}} = \frac{U_{\text{rG}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot I_{\text{kP}}} \tag{86} $$

$I_{\text{kP}}$ is the steady-state short-circuit current of a generator at a three-phase terminal short circuit. The value should be obtained from the manufacturer.

## 11.2.2 Steady-state short-circuit current of asynchronous motor or generator

With respect to Formula (105) of Table 4, the steady-state short-circuit current of an asynchronous motor/generator is zero in case of a three-phase short circuit at the terminals, Figure 9 and Formula (89).

## 11.2.3 Steady-state short-circuit current of wind power station unit with doubly fed asynchronous generator

The steady-state short-circuit currents $I_{\text{kWDmax}}$ and $I_{\text{kWDmin}}$ are to be provided by the manufacturer.

## 11.2.4 Steady-state short-circuit current of wind power station unit with full size converter

The steady-state short-circuit currents $I_{\text{kPFmax}}$ and $I_{\text{kPFmin}}$ are to be provided by the manufacturer.

## 11.2.5 Steady-state short-circuit current of network feeder

The steady-state short-circuit current is equal to the initial short-circuit current.

$$ I_{\text{k}} = I_{\text{k}}'' \tag{87} $$

## 11.2.6 Steady-state short-circuit current in case of multiple single-fed short circuits

The steady-state short-circuit current at the short-circuit location can be calculated by the summation of the individual steady-state short-circuit current contributions:

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

$$ I_{\mathrm{k}} = \sum_{i} I_{\mathrm{k}i} \tag{88} $$

Figure 9 example:

$$ I_{\mathrm{k}} = I_{\mathrm{kS}} + I_{\mathrm{kT}} + I_{\mathrm{kWF}} = \lambda I_{\mathrm{rGt}} + I_{\mathrm{kT}}^{\prime\prime} + I_{\mathrm{kWF}} \tag{89} $$

$\lambda$ ($\lambda_{\max}$ or $\lambda_{\min}$) is found from Figures 15 and 16. $I_{\mathrm{rGt}}$ is the rated current of the synchronous generator transferred to the high-voltage side (see 7.1.1) of the unit transformer in Figure 9.

In the case of network feeders or network feeders in series with transformers (see Figure 9) $I_{\mathrm{k}} = I_{\mathrm{k}}^{\prime\prime}$ is valid (far-from-generator short circuit).

## 11.2.7 Steady-state short-circuit current in case of multiple-fed short circuits

The steady-state short-circuit current may be calculated approximately by:

$$ I_{\mathrm{kmax}} = I_{\mathrm{bMO}} \tag{90} $$

$$ I_{\mathrm{kmin}} = I_{\mathrm{kmin}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{91} $$

$I_{\mathrm{bMO}}$ is calculated according to 9.1 without the influence of asynchronous motors/generators according to 7.1.2.

Formulas (90) and (91) are valid in the case of far-from-generator and near-to-generator short circuits.

## 11.3 Unbalanced short circuits

In all cases of unbalanced short circuits, the flux decay in the generator is not taken into account and the following formulas should be used:

$$ I_{\mathrm{k2}} = I_{\mathrm{k2}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{92} $$

$$ I_{\mathrm{k2E}} = I_{\mathrm{k2E}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{93} $$

$$ I_{\mathrm{kE2E}} = I_{\mathrm{kE2E}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{94} $$

$$ I_{\mathrm{k1}} = I_{\mathrm{k1}}^{\prime\prime} \tag{95} $$

In the case of maximum or minimum steady-state short circuits, the voltage factor $c_{\mathrm{max}}$ resp. $c_{\mathrm{min}}$ according to Table 1 shall be applied, see 7.1.2.

## 12 Short circuits at the low-voltage side of transformers, if one line conductor is interrupted at the high-voltage side

When fuses are used as incoming protection at the high-voltage side of network transformers, a short circuit at the secondary side may cause one fuse to clear before the other high-voltage lines.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

voltage fuses or a circuit-breaker eliminates the short circuit. This can lead to a situation where the partial short-circuit currents are too small to operate any further protection device, particularly in the case of minimum short-circuit currents. Electrical equipment may be overstressed due to the short-circuit duration.

Figure 17 describes this situation with balanced and unbalanced short circuits with earth connection at the short-circuit location F.

![Figure 17 - Transformer secondary short-circuits, if one line (fuse) is opened on the high-voltage side of a transformer Dyn5](img-35.jpeg)

**Figure 17 - Transformer secondary short-circuits, if one line (fuse) is opened on the high-voltage side of a transformer Dyn5**

The short-circuit currents, $I''_{\mathrm{kL1}}$, $I''_{\mathrm{kL2}}$, $I''_{\mathrm{kL3}}$ and $I''_{\mathrm{kN}}$ at the low-voltage side of the transformer in Figure 17 can be calculated using Formula (96) with the equivalent voltage source $c U_{\mathrm{n}} / \sqrt{3}$ at the short-circuit location F. The partial short-circuit currents $I''_{\mathrm{kL2HV}} = I''_{\mathrm{kL3HV}}$ at the high-voltage side in Figure 17 may also be calculated with Formula (96) using appropriate values for the factor $\alpha$. In all cases $I''_{\mathrm{k}\nu}$ is equal to $I'_{\mathrm{k}\nu}$, because the short circuits are far-from-generator short circuits (see 3.16 and Figure 1).

$$I''_{\mathrm{k}\nu} = \alpha \cdot \frac{c U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot \left| Z_{\mathrm{Qt}} + K_{\mathrm{T}} Z_{\mathrm{T}} + Z_{\mathrm{L}} + \beta \cdot \left( K_{\mathrm{T}} Z_{(0)\mathrm{T}} + Z_{(0)\mathrm{L}} \right) \right|} \tag{96}$$

where

$\nu$ represents L1, L2, L3, N(E) at the low-voltage side and L2 HV, L3 HV at the high-voltage side;

$Z_{\mathrm{Qt}} + K_{\mathrm{T}} Z_{\mathrm{T}} + Z_{\mathrm{L}}$ is the resultant impedance in the positive-sequence system at the LV-side ($Z_{\mathrm{T}} = Z_{\mathrm{TLV}}$);

$K_{\mathrm{T}} Z_{(0)\mathrm{T}} + Z_{(0)\mathrm{L}}$ is the resultant impedance in the zero-sequence system at the LV-side;

$\alpha, \beta$ are factors given in Table 3.

Any line-to-line short circuits without earth connection cause currents smaller than the rated currents, therefore this case is not taken into account in Table 3.

No short-circuit current on the low-voltage or on the high-voltage side of the transformer in Figure 17 is higher than the highest balanced or unbalanced short-circuit current in the case of an intact HV-feeding (see Figure 7). Therefore Formula (96) is normally of interest for the calculation of minimum short-circuit currents (see Table 1 for $c = c_{\min}$, and 7.1.1).

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

Table 3 - Factors $\alpha$ and $\beta$ for the calculation of short-circuit currents with Formula (96), rated transformation ratio $t_{\mathrm{r}} = U_{\mathrm{rTHV}}/U_{\mathrm{rTLV}}$

| Short-circuit in F (see Figure 17) | Three-phase short circuit | Line-to-line short circuit with earth connection | | Line-to-earth short circuit | | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | | Affected lines at the low-voltage side | L1, L2, L3 <br> L1, L2, L3, N(E) | L1, L3, N(E) | L1, L2, N(E) <br> L2, L3, N(E) | L2, N(E)$^{\text{a}}$ | | Factor $\beta$ | 0 | 2 | 0,5 | 0,5 | | Factor $\alpha$ (LV) for the currents | | | | | | $I''_{\mathrm{kL1}}$ | 0,5 | 1,5 | - | - | | $I''_{\mathrm{kL2}}$ | 1,0 | - | 1,5 | 1,5 | | $I''_{\mathrm{kL3}}$ | 0,5 | 1,5 | - | - | | $I''_{\mathrm{kN}}$ | - | 3,0 | 1,5 | 1,5 | | Factor $\alpha$ (HV) for the currents $I''_{kv}$ <br> $I''_{\mathrm{kL2HV}} = I''_{\mathrm{kL3HV}}$ | $\dfrac{1}{t_{\mathrm{r}}} \cdot \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ | $\dfrac{1}{t_{\mathrm{r}}} \cdot \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ | $\dfrac{1}{t_{\mathrm{r}}} \cdot \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ | $\dfrac{1}{t_{\mathrm{r}}} \cdot \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ |

$^{\text{a}}$ In the case of line-to-earth short circuits L1, N(E) or L3, N(E), the resulting small currents are stipulated by the transformer open-circuit impedances. They may be neglected.

## 13 Terminal short circuit of asynchronous motors

In the case of three-phase and line-to-line short circuits at the terminals of asynchronous motors, the partial short-circuit currents $I''_{\mathrm{kM}}$, $i_{\mathrm{pM}}$, $I_{\mathrm{bM}}$, and $I_{\mathrm{kM}}$ are evaluated as shown in Table 4. The influence of motors on the line-to-earth short-circuit current cannot be neglected in case of grounded systems. Take the impedances of the motors with $Z_{(1)\mathrm{M}} = Z_{(2)\mathrm{M}} = Z_{\mathrm{M}}$ and $Z_{(0)\mathrm{M}}$. If the star point of the motor is not earthed, the zero-sequence impedance becomes $Z_{(0)\mathrm{M}} = \infty$.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

**Table 4 - Calculation of short-circuit currents of asynchronous motors in the case of a short circuit at the terminals**

| Short circuit | Three-phase short circuit | Line-to-line short circuit | Line-to-earth short circuit | | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | | **Initial symmetrical short-circuit current** | $I_{\mathrm{k3M}}^{\prime\prime} = \dfrac{c U_{\mathrm{n}}}{\sqrt{3} \cdot Z_{\mathrm{M}}}$ (97) | $I_{\mathrm{k2M}}^{\prime\prime} = \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \cdot I_{\mathrm{k3M}}^{\prime\prime}$ (98) | See Clause 10 | | **Peak short-circuit current** | $i_{\mathrm{p3M}} = \kappa_{\mathrm{M}} \sqrt{2} \cdot I_{\mathrm{k3M}}^{\prime\prime}$ (99) | $i_{\mathrm{p2M}} = \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \cdot i_{\mathrm{p3M}}$ (100) | $i_{\mathrm{p1M}} = \kappa_{\mathrm{M}} \sqrt{2} \cdot I_{\mathrm{k1M}}^{\prime\prime}$ (101) | | | High-voltage motors:<br>$\kappa_{\mathrm{M}} = 1{,}65$ (corresponding to $R_{\mathrm{M}}/X_{\mathrm{M}} = 0{,}15$) for motor powers per pair of poles $< 1\ \text{MW}$<br>$\kappa_{\mathrm{M}} = 1{,}75$ (corresponding to $R_{\mathrm{M}}/X_{\mathrm{M}} = 0{,}10$) for motor powers per pair of poles $\ge 1\ \text{MW}$<br>Low-voltage motor groups with connection cables: $\kappa_{\mathrm{M}} = 1{,}3$ (corresponding to $R_{\mathrm{M}}/X_{\mathrm{M}} = 0{,}42$) | | | | **Symmetrical short-circuit breaking current** | $I_{\mathrm{b3M}} = \mu q \cdot I_{\mathrm{k3M}}^{\prime\prime}$ (102) | $I_{\mathrm{b2M}} \approx \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \cdot I_{\mathrm{k3M}}^{\prime\prime}$ (103) | $I_{\mathrm{b1M}} \approx I_{\mathrm{k1M}}^{\prime\prime}$ (104) | | | $\mu$ according to Formula (67) or Figure 13, with $I_{\mathrm{kM}}^{\prime\prime}/I_{\mathrm{rM}}$<br>$q$ according to Formula (69) or Figure 14. | | | | **Steady-state short-circuit current** | $I_{\mathrm{k3M}} = 0$ (105) | $I_{\mathrm{k2M}} \approx \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \cdot I_{\mathrm{k3M}}^{\prime\prime}$ (106) | $I_{\mathrm{k1M}} \approx I_{\mathrm{k1M}}^{\prime\prime}$ (107) |

## 14 Joule integral and thermal equivalent short-circuit current

The Joule integral $\int i^2 \,\mathrm{d}t$ is a measure of the energy generated in the resistive element of the system by the short-circuit current. In this standard it is calculated using a factor $m$ for the time-dependent heat effect of the DC component of the short-circuit current and a factor $n$ for the time-dependent heat effect of the AC component of the short-circuit current for one individual short circuit (see Figures 18 and 19).

$$\int_{0}^{T_{\mathrm{k}}} i^{2} \,\mathrm{d}t = \left(I_{\mathrm{k}}^{\prime\prime}\right)^{2} \cdot (m + n) \cdot T_{\mathrm{k}} = I_{\mathrm{th}}^{2} \cdot T_{\mathrm{k}} \tag{108}$$

The thermal equivalent short-circuit current is:

$$I_{\mathrm{th}} = I_{\mathrm{k}}^{\prime\prime} \sqrt{m + n} \tag{109}$$

For a series of $i$ three-phase successive individual short-circuit currents, the following formula shall be used for the calculation of the Joule integral or the thermal equivalent short-circuit current.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

$$ \int i^2 \mathrm{d}t = \sum_{i} \left(I_{\mathbf{k}i}^{"}\right)^2 \left(m_{i} + n_{i}\right) \cdot T_{\mathbf{k}i} = I_{\mathrm{th}}^2 \cdot T_{\mathbf{k}} \tag{110} $$

$$ I_{\mathrm{th}} = \sqrt{\frac{\int i^2 \mathrm{d}t}{T_{\mathbf{k}}}} \tag{111} $$

with $\quad T_{\mathbf{k}} = \sum_{i} T_{\mathbf{k}i}$ (112)

where

$I_{\mathbf{k}i}^{"}$ is the initial symmetrical three-phase short-circuit current for each short circuit;

$I_{\mathrm{th}}$ is the thermal equivalent short-circuit current;

$m_{i}$ is the factor for the heat effect of the DC component for each short-circuit current;

$n_{i}$ is the factor for the heat effect of the AC component for each short-circuit current;

$T_{\mathbf{k}i}$ is the duration of the short-circuit current for each short circuit;

$T_{\mathbf{k}}$ is the sum of the durations for each short-circuit current, see Formula (112).

The Joule integral and the thermal equivalent short-circuit current should always be given with the short-circuit duration with which they are associated.

> **[Figura]**: Figure 18 is a logarithmic-scale graph plotting the factor $m$ (vertical axis, ranging from 0 to 2) against the product $f \cdot T_k$ (horizontal axis, ranging from 0.5 to 500) on a log scale. The graph contains a family of curves, each corresponding to a different value of the factor $\kappa$, with labeled values $\kappa = 1{,}1$; $1{,}2$; $1{,}3$; $1{,}4$; $1{,}5$; $1{,}6$; $1{,}7$; $1{,}8$; $1{,}9$; and $1{,}95$. Each curve starts at a relatively high value of $m$ for small $f \cdot T_k$ and decreases monotonically toward zero as $f \cdot T_k$ increases. Higher $\kappa$ values correspond to higher $m$ values throughout the range. The curve for $\kappa = 1{,}95$ is the topmost and the curve for $\kappa = 1{,}1$ is the bottommost. All curves approach $m = 0$ asymptotically for large $f \cdot T_k$. A fine grid overlays the entire chart area.

**Figure 18 - Factor $m$ for the heat effect of the DC component of the short-circuit current (for programming, the formula to calculate $m$ is given in Annex A)**

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

> **[Figura]**: Graph of factor $n$ (y-axis, ranging from 0 to 1) versus short-circuit duration $T_\mathrm{k}$ (x-axis, ranging from 0,01 s to 10 s, logarithmic scale). Multiple curves are shown, each labelled by the ratio $\dot{I}_\mathrm{k} / I_\mathrm{k}$ = 1; 1,25; 1,5; 2; 2,5; 3; 4; 5; 6; 8; 10. For $\dot{I}_\mathrm{k} / I_\mathrm{k} = 1$ the curve remains at $n = 1$ across all durations. As the ratio increases, the curves drop more steeply with increasing $T_\mathrm{k}$, reaching lower values of $n$ at longer durations. All curves converge toward $n \approx 0$ at large $T_\mathrm{k}$.

**Figure 19 - Factor $n$ for the heat effect of the AC component of the short-circuit current (for programming, the formula to calculate $n$ is given in Annex A)**

The factors $m_i$ are obtained from Figure 18 using $f \cdot T_{\mathrm{k}i}$ and the factor $\kappa$ derived in 8.1. The factors $n_i$ are obtained from Figure 19 using $T_{\mathrm{k}i}$ and the quotient $\dot{I}_{\mathrm{k}i} / I_{\mathrm{k}i}$, where $I_{\mathrm{k}i}$ is the steady-state short-circuit current for each short circuit.

When a number of short circuits occur with a short time interval between them, the resulting Joule integral is the sum of the Joule integrals of the individual short-circuit currents, as given in Formula (110).

For distribution networks (far-from-generator short circuits) usually $n = 1$ can be used.

For far-from-generator short circuits with a rated short-circuit duration of 0,5 s or more, it is permissible to take $m + n = 1$.

If the Joule integral or the thermal equivalent short-circuit current shall be calculated for unbalanced short circuits, replace $\dot{I}_{\mathrm{k}i}$ with the appropriate unbalanced short-circuit currents.

NOTE For the calculation of the Joule integral or the thermal equivalent short-circuit current in three-phase AC systems, the three-phase short-circuit current may be decisive.

When a circuit is protected by fuses or current-limiting circuit-breakers, their Joule integral may limit the value below that calculated in accordance with Formula (108) or (110). In this case the Joule integral is determined from the characteristic of the current-limiting device.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

# Annex A

*(normative)*

# Formulas for the calculation of the factors $m$ and $n$

The factor $m$ in Figure 18 is given by:

$$m = \frac{1}{2 f T_{\mathrm{k}} \ln(\kappa - 1)} \cdot \left[ \mathrm{e}^{4 f T_{\mathrm{k}} \ln(\kappa - 1)} - 1 \right]$$

The factor $n$ in Figure 19 is given by:

$$\frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}''}{I_{\mathrm{k}}} = 1: \quad n = 1$$

$$\frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}''}{I_{\mathrm{k}}} \geq 1{,}25 :$$

$$\begin{aligned} n = \frac{1}{\left(I_{\mathrm{k}}'' / I_{\mathrm{k}}\right)^{2}} \cdot \Bigg[ & 1 + \frac{T_{\mathrm{d}}'}{20 T_{\mathrm{k}}} \cdot \left(1 - \mathrm{e}^{-20 T_{\mathrm{k}} / T_{\mathrm{d}}'}\right) \cdot \left(\frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}''}{I_{\mathrm{k}}} - \frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}'}{I_{\mathrm{k}}}\right)^{2} \\ & + \frac{T_{\mathrm{d}}'}{2 T_{\mathrm{k}}} \cdot \left(1 - \mathrm{e}^{-2 T_{\mathrm{k}} / T_{\mathrm{d}}'}\right) \cdot \left(\frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}'}{I_{\mathrm{k}}} - 1\right)^{2} \\ & + \frac{T_{\mathrm{d}}'}{5 T_{\mathrm{k}}} \cdot \left(1 - \mathrm{e}^{-10 T_{\mathrm{k}} / T_{\mathrm{d}}'}\right) \cdot \left(\frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}''}{I_{\mathrm{k}}} - \frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}'}{I_{\mathrm{k}}}\right) \\ & + \frac{2 T_{\mathrm{d}}'}{T_{\mathrm{k}}} \cdot \left(1 - \mathrm{e}^{-T_{\mathrm{k}} / T_{\mathrm{d}}'}\right) \cdot \left(\frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}'}{I_{\mathrm{k}}} - 1\right)^{2} \\ & + \frac{T_{\mathrm{d}}'}{5{,}5 T_{\mathrm{k}}} \cdot \left(1 - \mathrm{e}^{-11 T_{\mathrm{k}} / T_{\mathrm{d}}'}\right) \cdot \left(\frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}''}{I_{\mathrm{k}}} - \frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}'}{I_{\mathrm{k}}}\right) \cdot \left(\frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}'}{I_{\mathrm{k}}} - 1\right) \Bigg] \end{aligned}$$

where

$$\frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}'}{I_{\mathrm{k}}} = \frac{I_{\mathrm{k}}'' / I_{\mathrm{k}}}{0{,}88 + 0{,}17 \cdot I_{\mathrm{k}}'' / I_{\mathrm{k}}}$$

$$T_{\mathrm{d}}' = \frac{3{,}1\ \mathrm{s}}{I_{\mathrm{k}}' / I_{\mathrm{k}}}$$

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

# Annex B (informative)

# Nodal admittance and nodal impedance matrices

The nodal admittance matrix for the positive-sequence system of a network with $n$ nodes has the following $n \times n$ structure:

$$ \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)} = \left[ \begin{array}{ccccccc} & 1 & 2 & \dots & i & \dots & n \\ 1 & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)11} & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)12} & \dots & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)1i} & \dots & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)1n} \\ 2 & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)21} & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)22} & \dots & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)2i} & \dots & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)2n} \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ i & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)i1} & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)i2} & \dots & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)ii} & \dots & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)in} \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ n & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)n1} & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)n2} & \dots & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)ni} & \dots & \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)nn} \end{array} \right] $$

The mutual admittance between the nodes $i$ and $j$ is the sum of the short-circuit admittances of the electrical equipment (see Clause 6) between the nodes $i$ and $j$ (see Figure B.1):

$$ \underline{Y}_{(1)ij} = \sum_{\ell = 1}^{m} \frac{1}{Z_{(1)ij,\ell}} $$

The self-admittances of the node $i$ (and similarly for the other nodes) is the negative sum of the admittances in the $i^{\text{th}}$ line and the negative sum of the short-circuit admittances of the electrical equipment between the nodes $i$ and the reference node 0 of the positive-sequence system.

$$ \underline{Y}_{(1)ii} = - \sum_{j = 1, j \neq i}^{n} \underline{Y}_{(1)ij} - \sum_{s = 1}^{k} \frac{1}{Z_{(1)i0,s}} $$

The structure of the nodal matrices for the negative-sequence system and the zero-sequence system are similar.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

> **[Figura]**: Schematic circuit diagram illustrating the formulation of the nodal admittance matrix. Two buses *i* and *j* are shown connected by *m* parallel branch impedances $\underline{Z}_{(1)ij,1}$ through $\underline{Z}_{(1)ij,m}$, each represented as a series impedance element (box with tilde symbol). Bus *i* also has shunt impedances $\underline{Z}_{(1)i0,1}$ through $\underline{Z}_{(1)i0,k}$ connecting it to the reference bus $0_{(1)}$, and bus *j* similarly has shunt impedances connecting to the same reference bus. All shunt elements are shown as boxes with the numeral 2 (representing half-value splits). The reference bus $0_{(1)}$ runs horizontally at the bottom of the diagram.

**Figure B.1 - Formulation of the nodal admittance matrix**

The nodal impedance matrix for the positive-sequence system is the inverse matrix of the nodal admittance matrix for the positive-sequence system with the following $n \times n$ structure.

$$ \underline{\mathbf{Z}}_{(1)} = \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(1)}^{-1} = \begin{bmatrix} \underline{Z}_{(1)11} & \underline{Z}_{(1)12} & \cdots & \underline{Z}_{(1)1i} & \cdots & \underline{Z}_{(1)1n} \\ \underline{Z}_{(1)21} & \underline{Z}_{(1)22} & \cdots & \underline{Z}_{(1)2i} & \cdots & \underline{Z}_{(1)2n} \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ \underline{Z}_{(1)i1} & \underline{Z}_{(1)i2} & \cdots & \underline{Z}_{(1)ii} & \cdots & \underline{Z}_{(1)in} \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ \underline{Z}_{(1)n1} & \underline{Z}_{(1)n2} & \cdots & \underline{Z}_{(1)ni} & \cdots & \underline{Z}_{(1)nn} \end{bmatrix} $$

The nodal impedances of the negative-sequence system and the zero-sequence system are found in the same manner:

$$\underline{\mathbf{Z}}_{(2)} = \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(2)}^{-1}$$

$$\underline{\mathbf{Z}}_{(0)} = \underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{(0)}^{-1}$$

An example is given by Figure B.2 with the impedances of Table B.1.

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

> **[Figura]**: Single-line schematic of an example power network at 110 kV. On the left, a power station unit S comprises a generator G (3~) connected through transformer T1 to busbar A. Two parallel overhead lines L1 and L2 connect busbar A to busbar C (right side), where the network infeed Q (represented by a cross-hatched box) is connected. From busbar A, transformer T2 feeds busbar B, to which a motor group M (3~) is connected. From busbar C, transformer T3 feeds a wind farm with PF (power-factor) units, connected at the bottom right. All components are labeled with their identifiers. The voltage level is 110 kV.

**Figure B.2 - Example**

### Table B.1 - Impedances of electrical equipment referred to the 110 kV side

| Equipment | Positive-sequence system | Negative-sequence system | Zero-sequence system | | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | | **Q** | $\underline{Z}_{\text{Q}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(2)\text{Q}} = \underline{Z}_{\text{Q}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{Q}}$ | | **S** | $\underline{Z}_{\text{SK}} = K_{\text{S}}\!\left(t_{rT1}^{2}\,\underline{Z}_{\text{G}} + \underline{Z}_{\text{T1HV}}\right)$ | $\underline{Z}_{(2)\text{SK}} = \underline{Z}_{\text{SK}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{SK}} = K_{\text{S}}\,\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{T1HV}}$ | | **M** | $\underline{Z}_{\text{Mt}} = t_{rT2}^{2}\,\underline{Z}_{\text{M}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(2)\text{Mt}} = \underline{Z}_{\text{Mt}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{Mt}} = \infty$ | | **PF** | $\underline{Z}_{\text{PFt}} = \infty$ | $\underline{Z}_{(2)\text{PFt}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{PFt}}^{\,\text{a}}$ | | **T2** | $\underline{Z}_{\text{T2K}} = K_{\text{T}}\,\underline{Z}_{\text{T2HV}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(2)\text{T2K}} = \underline{Z}_{\text{T2K}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{T2K}} = K_{\text{T}}\,\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{T2}}$ | | **T3** | $\underline{Z}_{\text{T3K}} = K_{\text{T}}\,\underline{Z}_{\text{T3HV}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(2)\text{T3K}} = \underline{Z}_{\text{T3K}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{T3K}} = K_{\text{T}}\,\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{T3}}$ | | **L1** | $\underline{Z}_{\text{L1}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(2)\text{L1}} = \underline{Z}_{\text{L1}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{L1}}$ | | **L2** | $\underline{Z}_{\text{L2}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(2)\text{L2}} = \underline{Z}_{\text{L2}}$ | $\underline{Z}_{(0)\text{L2}}$ |

$^{\text{a}}$ Depends on the vector group of the transformer.

---

Nodal admittance matrix for the positive-sequence system:

$$ \underline{Y}_{(1)} = \begin{bmatrix} -\dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{L1}}} - \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{L2}}} - \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{T2K}}} - \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{SK}}} & \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{L1}}} + \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{L2}}} & \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{T2K}}} & 0 \\[10pt] \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{L1}}} + \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{L2}}} & -\dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{L1}}} - \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{L2}}} - \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{T3K}}} - \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{Q}}} & 0 & \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{T3K}}} \\[10pt] \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{T2K}}} & 0 & -\dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{T2K}}} - \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{Mt}}} & 0 \\[10pt] 0 & \dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{T3K}}} & 0 & -\dfrac{1}{Z_{\text{T3K}}} \end{bmatrix} $$

---

Nodal admittance matrix for the negative-sequence system:

$$ \underline{Y}^{(2)} = \underline{Y}^{(1)} $$

<!-- PAGE_BREAK -->

Nodal admittance matrix for the negative-sequence system:

$$ \underline{Y}_{(2)} = \begin{bmatrix} -\left(\frac{1}{Z_{(2)L1}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(2)L2}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(2)T2K}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(2)SK}}\right) & \frac{1}{Z_{(2)L1}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(2)L2}} & \frac{1}{Z_{(2)T2K}} & 0 \\ \frac{1}{Z_{(2)L1}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(2)L2}} & -\left(\frac{1}{Z_{(2)L1}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(2)L2}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(2)T3K}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(2)Q}}\right) & 0 & \frac{1}{Z_{(2)T3K}} \\ \frac{1}{Z_{(2)T2K}} & 0 & -\left(\frac{1}{Z_{(2)T2K}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(2)Mt}}\right) & 0 \\ 0 & \frac{1}{Z_{(2)T3K}} & 0 & -\left(\frac{1}{Z_{(2)T3K}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(2)PPt}}\right) \end{bmatrix} $$

Nodal admittance matrix for the zero-sequence system:

$$ \underline{Y}_{(0)} = \begin{bmatrix} -\left(\frac{1}{Z_{(0)L1}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(0)L2}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(0)T2K}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(0)SK}}\right) & \frac{1}{Z_{(0)L1}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(0)L2}} & \frac{1}{Z_{(0)T2K}} & 0 \\ \frac{1}{Z_{(0)L1}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(0)L2}} & -\left(\frac{1}{Z_{(0)L1}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(0)L2}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(0)T3K}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(0)Q}}\right) & 0 & \frac{1}{Z_{(0)T3K}} \\ \frac{1}{Z_{(0)T2K}} & 0 & -\frac{1}{Z_{(0)T2K}} & 0 \\ 0 & \frac{1}{Z_{(0)T3K}} & 0 & -\left(\frac{1}{Z_{(0)T3K}} + \frac{1}{Z_{(0)PPt}}\right) \end{bmatrix} $$

The short-circuit impedances of the positive-sequence, negative-sequence and zero-sequence systems are the negative diagonal elements $Z_{(1)ii}$, $Z_{(2)ii}$, and $Z_{(0)ii}$ of the nodal impedance matrices:

$$ Z_{(1)} = -Z_{(1)ii} $$

$$ Z_{(2)} = -Z_{(2)ii} $$

$$ Z_{(0)} = -Z_{(0)ii} $$

# COMMISSION ÉLECTROTECHNIQUE INTERNATIONALE

## COURANTS DE COURT-CIRCUIT DANS LES RÉSEAUX TRIPHASÉS À COURANT ALTERNATIF -

### Partie 0: Calcul des courants

### AVANT-PROPOS

1) La Commission Electrotechnique Internationale (IEC) est une organisation mondiale de normalisation composée de l'ensemble des comités électrotechniques nationaux (Comités nationaux de l'IEC). L'IEC a pour objet de favoriser la coopération internationale pour toutes les questions de normalisation dans les domaines de l'électricité et de l'électronique. A cet effet, l'IEC - entre autres activités - publie des Normes internationales, des Spécifications techniques, des Rapports techniques, des Spécifications accessibles au public (PAS) et des Guides (ci-après dénommés "Publication(s) de l'IEC"). Leur élaboration est confiée à des comités d'études, aux travaux desquels tout Comité national intéressé par le sujet traité peut participer. Les organisations internationales, gouvernementales et non gouvernementales, en liaison avec l'IEC, participent également aux travaux. L'IEC collabore étroitement avec l'Organisation Internationale de Normalisation (ISO), selon des conditions fixées par accord entre les deux organisations.

2) Les décisions ou accords officiels de l'IEC concernant les questions techniques représentent, dans la mesure du possible, un accord international sur les sujets étudiés, étant donné que les Comités nationaux de l'IEC intéressés sont représentés dans chaque comité d'études.

3) Les Publications de l'IEC se présentent sous la forme de recommandations internationales et sont agréées comme telles par les Comités nationaux de l'IEC. Tous les efforts raisonnables sont entrepris afin que l'IEC s'assure de l'exactitude du contenu technique de ses publications; l'IEC ne peut pas être tenue responsable de l'éventuelle mauvaise utilisation ou interprétation qui en est faite par un quelconque utilisateur final.

4) Dans le but d'encourager l'uniformité internationale, les Comités nationaux de l'IEC s'engagent, dans toute la mesure possible, à appliquer de façon transparente les Publications de l'IEC dans leurs publications nationales et régionales. Toutes divergences entre toutes Publications de l'IEC et toutes publications nationales ou régionales correspondantes doivent être indiquées en termes clairs dans ces dernières.

5) L'IEC elle-même ne fournit aucune attestation de conformité. Des organismes de certification indépendants fournissant des services d'évaluation de conformité et, dans certains secteurs, accèdent aux marques de conformité de l'IEC. L'IEC n'est responsable d'aucun des services effectués par les organismes de certification indépendants.

6) Tous les utilisateurs doivent s'assurer qu'ils sont en possession de la dernière édition de cette publication.

7) Aucune responsabilité ne doit être imputée à l'IEC, à ses administrateurs, employés, auxiliaires ou mandataires, y compris ses experts particuliers et les membres de ses comi
